\item oracle exactness at city scale is checked against an independent oracle, not proved.
\end{enumerate}
Further proxies are the sampled customer zones instead of whole-city demand, equal market shares in seven cities, the slice multiplicity, and a \emph{broad} social cost that adds the delay imposed on background traffic (population times vehicle ownership times one hour per day, valued at the wage, times the added slowdown, divided by $\varsigma_z$). The broad objective is reported but not used for conclusions.

\paragraph{Instances as a benchmark} The pipeline (extract download, zones, travel times, depots, parameters with grades, p-box construction, game instances with seeds) is in the repository and regenerates all instances from the raw extracts. The instances are small (22 customer zones per carrier, four to six carriers) so that exact oracles are possible; they are intended as a common test bed for algorithms on multi-carrier routing games, not as validated city models.

\section{Computational study}\label{sec:results}

\paragraph{Research questions}
\begin{description}[itemsep=0pt]
\item[RQ1] Is BRL pricing exact, what does its completion window save, and how far does branch-and-price scale? (Section~\ref{sec:res-brl})
\item[RQ2] Why probability boxes: what does the p-box criterion cost and buy out of sample, compared with sample-average, Wasserstein and CVaR criteria? (Section~\ref{sec:res-pbox})
\item[RQ3] How do equilibria computed with an exact oracle differ from those with price-and-branch, and how do the engine's components compare? (Section~\ref{sec:res-engine})
\item[RQ4] What do the equilibria, the grid leader, the price of anarchy and the imprecision effects look like on the nine cities, and how stable are they over seeds? (Section~\ref{sec:res-eq})
\item[RQ5] How do DIP and ARP compare with the grid, and with the social optimum, in oracle calls and gain? (Section~\ref{sec:res-leader})
\item[RQ6] Are the findings robust to the carriers' criterion? (Section~\ref{sec:res-crit})
\item[RQ7] Do the theoretical bounds hold on the city equilibria, and does vehicle-count congestion break pure equilibria in practice? (Section~\ref{sec:res-theory})
\end{description}

\paragraph{Protocol and seeds} All numbers are generated from the result files of the repository by \texttt{make\_tables.py}. A seed redraws the depots, the customer zones of each carrier, the travel-time sectors and the 30 observed days, hence the probability boxes; calibration inputs are the same in every seed. Seeds 0--2 were run for all nine cities and the exact-oracle study was extended to seeds 3--9, which used 55\,046 oracle calls, of which 1.1\% were not proven optimal. Coverage is as follows.
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*,itemsep=0pt]
\item New Delhi, Buenos Aires, London: ten seeds for everything, including the full 63-point exact grid and ARP.
\item Cairo, Moscow, New York, Mumbai, Paris, Berlin: 9 seeds (0--8) for the unpriced equilibrium, the social optimum, the price of anarchy and DIP. Grid-derived quantities (congestion-only, delivery-only and combined gains, delivery share, priced gap, DIP against the grid) still have three seeds and a reduced exact grid (7--17 screened points per seed), so they are lower bounds (marked $^*$). ARP was run for Mumbai (five seeds) and not for the other five.
\item Imprecision sweeps (three seeds): all 12 widths for New Delhi, Buenos Aires, Mumbai, London; 6 widths for Cairo, Moscow, New York, Paris, Berlin.
\item Still computed with price-and-branch: the engine comparison (Table~\ref{tab:e5}), the ZMC checks, the theory tests (Section~\ref{sec:res-theory}), the tiny-game verification, the DIP comparisons for Berlin seed 2, Moscow seed 2, New York seed 2 (price-and-branch grids), the social-optimum benchmark of the broad objective in New York, Moscow and Berlin, and the p-box reference runs of the criterion study (Section~\ref{sec:res-crit}).
\end{itemize}
Means over seeds are given with percentile bootstrap 95\% confidence intervals (10{,}000 resamples of the seeds). They describe the spread over sampled slices of a city, not the uncertainty in the calibration inputs, which Table~\ref{tab:sens} examines separately. Of the 41{,}469 oracle calls of the seeds 0--2 study, 99.17\% were proven optimal, and the remaining gap of an unproven call was at most 14 USD. Every reported equilibrium is a pure Nash equilibrium of the game with the exact oracle up to unproven calls (Section~\ref{sec:res-verif}).

\subsection{BRL and branch-and-price}\label{sec:res-brl}

\paragraph{Exactness} The upper-prevision formula agrees with a linear program over all joint laws on 40 random instances to a relative error of $4.5\cdot10^{-14}$; the comonotone worst case exceeds the value under independent worst marginals by 4.5\% on average and up to 14.1\%. BRL pricing equals complete enumeration on every test instance, including 18 of 18 sub-instances built from the nine cities. Fixing one risk level for all routes is suboptimal in 9 of 20 small exact comparisons, by up to 6.42\% (Proposition~\ref{prop:fixedu}).

\paragraph{Public instances: setup} We also ran BRL on Solomon (25, 50, 100 customers) and Augerat A and B instances. They are treated as capacitated routing with a soft duration limit and four sectors with lognormal common-factor uncertainty. This is an emulation. It is not a reproduction of published branch-and-cut results, and the instances of Zhang et al.\ and Adulyasak and Jaillet could not be obtained.

\paragraph{What BRL does well: label counts} Table~\ref{tab:brl-pricing} compares labelling rules on random instances ($L=33$ levels, nine sectors). BRL takes 0.004--0.065 seconds. Sector dominance times out from the smallest size, and quantile-vector and scenario labelling from $n=15$. These random instances are favourable to BRL, and the reductions on real data are smaller (Table~\ref{tab:brl-labels}).

With the duals of the root column generation, the window rule keeps 1.8--2.9 times fewer labels than the same stop-loss comparison without the window. It keeps 2.8--6.3 times fewer than quantile-vector dominance on the metro instances (22--80 customer zones). On the public benchmark instances the factors are 1.6--14 and 2.7--23. The ratios shrink as $n$ grows and are lower bounds where a rule hit its comparison cap. On the public instances scenario labelling keeps 61\,123--98\,930 labels where BRL keeps 13\,175--15\,455.

We have not run a general resource-extension-function or function-label labelling code \citep{Dabia2013} against the completion window; \citet{Irnich2008} reports no computational study.

\paragraph{What BRL does well: proof rates} On the nine metro instances with 22 customer zones, branch-and-price proved optimality in 9 of nine cities, in at most 18 minutes on one core (Moscow, 7487 nodes; Table~\ref{tab:brl-metro}). Against complete enumeration on 800 random formulations, branch-and-price returned the optimum in 800 of 800. Price-and-branch was strictly suboptimal in 39 of them, by up to 7.3\%.

On the public instances, over 51 cells (instance, number of training days, draw), the BRL solve was proven optimal in 59\% of cases. The figures are 35\% for the deterministic plan, 20\% for SAA, 3.9\% for the Wasserstein criterion and 0\% for mean-CVaR (90 s limit, same branch-and-price). BRL was proven on Solomon-25 in a median of 3.8 s (27 cells).

\paragraph{What BRL does well: scale} The scale study (Table~\ref{tab:brl-scale}) gives proofs for Cairo, London and New York at 30 zones, and for London at 40 zones (and for New York at 40 zones with the device configuration). Heuristic-first pricing gave a median 2.2-fold speed-up with an identical LP bound; the completion bound gave none.

\paragraph{What BRL does not do: held-out cost} On held-out days from the same law, BRL does not beat the sample-average plan (Table~\ref{tab:brl-oos}). Its plans cost 0.30--5.0\% more on the mean and 1.2--2.8\% more at the 95th percentile after polishing. After a local-search polish of the best plan of any arm, all arms are within 0.17\% of the best known plan under their own criterion. Only in a stress test, with a law shifted away from the training days and ten training days, is BRL's 95th percentile 2.3\% lower and its CVaR$_{0.9}$ 2.5\% lower, at 3.3\% more on the mean. The advantage vanishes at 30 or more days. BRL's advantage is thus proof speed and label counts against scenario and quantile labelling, not out-of-sample cost.

\paragraph{What BRL does not do: Augerat instances and larger sizes} BRL was proven in only 0 of 15 Augerat cells, and not for Augerat A-n32-k5 (gap 0.25\% with 63\,047 columns and no cuts). Optimality was not proven at London with 60 or 80 zones or New Delhi with 60. The method should therefore be read as exact where proven (22 customer zones in all nine cities, 30 to 40 customers in London and New York) and not beyond about 40 customers.

\paragraph{Conservatism} On the metro instances the robust optimum is not the best plan under the calibrated law: evaluated under it, the branch-and-price plan is worse than the sample-average plan in Buenos Aires (+7.0\%), New York (+4.8\%), Berlin (+6.8\%), and its worst-case cost lies between 10\% and 45\% above its expected cost under the law. The next subsection places this in context.

\subsection{Why probability boxes}\label{sec:res-pbox}

A natural question is why to use DKW probability boxes with unknown dependence instead of sample-average approximation (SAA), a type-1 Wasserstein ball or CVaR. We compared the criteria out of sample on the nine metro instances (seed 0, 22 customer zones), with $n=10,20,30,60$ training days, 20 independent draws, and a held-out set of 20{,}000 days of the instance's law. The planner is a heuristic (Clarke--Wright, local search, ruin and recreate, and a set-partitioning step over all evaluated routes), validated against branch-and-price: optimal in 8 of 9 instances, at most 0.10\% above in the others (Paris). Table~\ref{tab:ambig} gives the results.

\begin{table}[tbp]\centering\small
\caption{Out-of-sample comparison of criteria on the nine metro instances (mean over 20 draws and cities). Regret: expected cost of the plan above the best known plan for the true law, \%. Other columns at $n=20$: probability that at least one route runs overtime on a day; CVaR$_{0.95}$ of the daily plan cost above the best known expected cost; criterion value minus realised expected cost.}\label{tab:ambig}
\shrinkbox{\begin{tabular}{lrrrrrr}
\toprule
Criterion & regret \% $n=10$ & $n=20$ & $n=60$ & P(overtime day), $n=20$ & CVaR$_{0.95}$ above best, \% ($n=20$) & criterion minus true cost, \% ($n=20$) \\
\midrule
SAA & 0.38 & 0.27 & 0.16 & 0.15 & 38.8 & -0.086 \\
Wasserstein, cross-validated radius & 0.43 & 0.26 & 0.15 & 0.14 & 39.1 & 3.2 \\
Wasserstein, theory radius & 1.5 & 1.1 & 0.51 & 0.16 & 41.1 & 9.5 \\
CVaR$_{0.9}$ & 2.4 & 2.5 & 2.9 & 0.063 & 34.6 & 19 \\
p-box, unknown dependence, 0.80 & 6.0 & 4.6 & 1.6 & 0.069 & 33.9 & 29 \\
p-box, unknown dependence, 0.95 & 7.9 & 5.1 & 2.6 & 0.070 & 34.5 & 35 \\
p-box, unknown dependence, 0.99 & 8.9 & 6.3 & 4.1 & 0.077 & 35.8 & 40 \\
p-box, independent, 0.95 & 5.9 & 3.8 & 1.6 & 0.053 & 31.9 & 29 \\
comonotone, empirical marginals (no band) & 0.31 & 0.090 & 0.051 & 0.13 & 37.5 & 5.3 \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
}
\end{table}

\emph{The p-box loses in expected cost.} At every $n\le60$ its regret is 1.6--8.9\% (for confidence 0.80 to 0.99, falling with $n$), against 0.27\% for SAA at $n=20$ and 0.16\% at $n=60$. Wasserstein with a cross-validated radius reproduces SAA (0.26\% and 0.15\%); at the theory radius it costs 1.1\% and 0.51\%; CVaR$_{0.9}$ costs 2.5\% and 2.9\%. The cost comes from the DKW band, not from the unknown-dependence worst case: the comonotone criterion with empirical marginals and no band has regret 0.090\% and 0.051\%.

\emph{It buys tail protection and a bound.} At $n=20$ the probability of an overtime day is 0.15 for SAA and 0.069 for the p-box with confidence 0.80 (CVaR$_{0.9}$: 0.063), and the CVaR$_{0.95}$ of the daily cost is 3.6--5.0 percentage points lower than under SAA at every $n$. Under a common additive congestion shock at test time, the p-box plan with confidence 0.80 has the same expected cost as the SAA plan at a shock of about 0.50 ($n=60$) to 0.60 ($n=20$) multiplier units (multipliers of the instances are about 1.4--1.6) and is cheaper beyond (2.7\% at a shock of 1.0, $n=60$). Its criterion value is an upper bound on the true expected cost whenever the DKW band holds: the criterion exceeds the realised cost by 20--40\% for the p-box, by 3.2\% for the cross-validated Wasserstein criterion and by -0.086\% for SAA at $n=20$. This is the conservatism referred to above.

\emph{What the criterion provides.} P-boxes do not minimise expected cost. They provide a guaranteed upper bound that shrinks with the sample size, and tail protection, at an expected-cost price that is 1.6--8.9\% for $n\le60$. Which criterion the carriers use matters for the game results; Section~\ref{sec:res-crit} quantifies that.

\subsection{Equilibrium engine and exactness of the oracle}\label{sec:res-engine}

\paragraph{Effect of the inexact integer step} The seeds 0--2 experiments were first computed with price-and-branch. Tested against a fresh exact oracle, those base equilibria were exact in New Delhi, Buenos Aires, Mumbai, London in every seed but not in 10 of the 27 city-seeds, where a carrier could gain at most 4.0 USD per day with a plan the pooled integer program had not generated. City-mean welfare changes by at most 0.14\% outside Buenos Aires and by -1.4\% in Buenos Aires, where seed 0 moves to a different equilibrium (-5.4\%); single seeds elsewhere change by up to 0.53\% with both signs. The exact oracle changes which equilibrium is reached rather than improving welfare systematically; it takes three to twelve times longer.

\paragraph{Engine components} On the city games (Table~\ref{tab:e5}, price-and-branch oracle) ZMC uses 36--65 calls, about ten per carrier, and its potential gap coincides with those of Frank--Wolfe and column generation with eight rounds to three digits in most cities. Plain best response needs 8--17 calls and is as good as ZMC (within $10^{-3}$) in Cairo, Moscow, New York, Paris, Berlin and worse in New Delhi, Buenos Aires, Mumbai, London. ZMC therefore does not beat best response on these games: only 32--89 coordinates are active, so the dimension that favours ZMC in the synthetic instances is absent. Its dimension independence is not tested at city scale.

\subsection{Unpriced equilibria and the price of anarchy}\label{sec:res-eq}\label{sec:res-poa}

The cities fall into two groups (Table~\ref{tab:e1}, seeds 0--2, exact oracle). In Cairo, Moscow, New York, Paris, Berlin the carriers serve at least 85\% of customer demand; in New Delhi, Buenos Aires, Mumbai, London they serve 18--45\%, because the delivery fee is low relative to the vehicle-hour cost or the shift is short. The added slowdown caused by delivery vehicles is 0.77--17.6\% (Moscow highest).

\paragraph{Price of anarchy over ten seeds} The social optimum is approximated by column generation on the convexified welfare problem followed by rounding and polish. The equilibria considered are those found by the engine and by best response from the empty and full-service profiles and from three random price vectors. The price of anarchy is therefore \emph{empirical}: a ratio of the rounded optimum to the equilibria found, not a bound over all equilibria. Table~\ref{tab:seeds10} gives the ten-seed results. Among the engine's equilibria the price of anarchy is at least 1.10 in some seed only in New Delhi, Buenos Aires, London. Its mean is 1.53 in London, 1.38 in Buenos Aires and 1.18 in New Delhi, with ranges 1.25--1.94, 1.28--1.46 and 1.09--1.31. In the other cities it is at most 1.06 in mean (Mumbai's worst equilibrium found has mean 1.10 [1.08, 1.13]). The means are ordered London $>$ Buenos Aires $>$ New Delhi, but the ordering holds seed by seed only in 7 of 10 seeds: London is below Buenos Aires in seeds 4, 6, 7, and Buenos Aires exceeds New Delhi in 10 of 10. With the grid's best charges the equilibrium is much closer to the optimum, with a priced gap of at most 7.4\% in any seed. The relaxation bound exceeds the rounded optimum by up to 25\% (London), so the true price of anarchy could be larger. Table~\ref{tab:e3} (Appendix) gives the seeds 0--2 detail.

\paragraph{Imprecision} Two kinds of imprecision are varied (seeds 0--2; Table~\ref{tab:e4}, Figure~\ref{fig:e4}). Widening the travel-time probability boxes from $\varepsilon=0$ to $\varepsilon=0.41$ acts through the BRL route costs: deliveries fall by 0--78\% and welfare by 2.7--82\% at the widest box. Cairo loses no deliveries, Moscow and New York a few per cent, Paris and Berlin somewhat more, Mumbai more, and New Delhi, Buenos Aires and London about half or more; the low-service magnitudes vary by seed (London: -60.0 / -56.1 / -78.2\% in seeds 0, 1, 2). Widening the credal width about rivals under $\Gamma$-maximin from $\delta=0$ to $\delta=0.80$ lowers deliveries by 31--100\% and welfare by 22--100\%. Deliveries are not always monotone in the width: 3 of 54 series contain a rise, all in Buenos Aires; Proposition~\ref{prop:cs} does not apply to $\Gamma$-maximin or to a single equilibrium that can switch. Imprecision acts like a tax on activity; because vehicle-hours are proportional to deliveries (S3), the model cannot separate a congestion effect from a delivery effect. The imprecision sweeps were not extended to ten seeds.

\subsection{Leader methods: grid, DIP and ARP}\label{sec:res-leader}

\paragraph{The grid} The baseline leader chooses a congestion charge per vehicle-hour, $t_z=\vartheta\gamma c_zX_z^{\rm NE}$ with $\vartheta\in\{0,0.5,1,2,4,8,16\}$, and a per-delivery charge $s\in\{-0.6,\dots,0.6\}$ times the fee (negative: subsidy), a two-parameter family of 63 points with one warm-started equilibrium each. Table~\ref{tab:seeds10} and Table~\ref{tab:e2} (Appendix, seeds 0--2) give the reduction of social cost for the best exact point using only the congestion charge, only the delivery charge, or both.

\emph{Congestion charge alone.} It reaches at least 1.7\% of the social cost in 4 of 10 seeds in Buenos Aires (maximum 6.5\%), 2 of 10 in London (maximum 5.4\%) and 1 of 10 in New Delhi (maximum 2.9\%). In the seeds 0--2 exact run the Buenos Aires seed-0 case (6.51\%) arose because the exact oracle selected a worse unpriced equilibrium (welfare 252.2 against 266.7 USD with price-and-branch). The comparison is stable: the mean congestion-only gain is 0.32--2.4\% in the three full-grid cities, against 12.1--39.8\% for the delivery charge alone.

\emph{Per-delivery instrument.} In every case with a material gain the best point has a subsidy. The delivery charge alone achieves 0.81 [0.69, 0.92] of the combined gain in New Delhi, 0.98 [0.96, 0.99] in Buenos Aires and 0.82 [0.75, 0.88] in London (ratio of the per-seed shares; bootstrap 95\% CI), and below one half only in New Delhi seed 0. Mumbai's share is not established (3 seeds, CI 0.46--1.4). The gain is material only where the unpriced equilibrium under-delivers; in Cairo, Moscow and New York it is at most 0.46\%. In London the combined gain ranges widely over seeds. The mechanism visible in the model is under-provision caused by fixed route costs and market power, which a subsidy corrects, while the congestion externality among carriers is small; within a zone the two instruments are collinear (S3). Subsidies cost the city money; since charges are transfers in the welfare definition, this fiscal cost is not part of the social cost. With the broad objective the leader's best response in New Delhi, Mumbai, London is to stop deliveries, an artifact of the proxy that supports no conclusion (Table~\ref{tab:e2broad}, Appendix).

\subsubsection{DIP against the grid and against the social optimum}\label{sec:res-dip}

DIP was run with the exact oracle on all nine cities, seeds 0--8 or 0--9 (Table~\ref{tab:seeds10b}). Calls count every routing-oracle query of each method with the same counter. DIP searches a richer family than the grid (one charge per zone against two parameters), so a gain share above one against the grid is not a like-for-like win; restricted to the grid's family, DIP reaches on average 1.0 of the grid gain in the seeds 0--2 runs, with the worst case 0.50 (Mumbai, seed 0, a reduced grid). Table~\ref{tab:seeds10b} therefore also reports every method's gain as a share of the gain of the rounded social optimum.

Shares are not reported where the optimum's gain is below 0.5\% of $|$social cost$|$.}\label{tab:seeds10b}

\paragraph{Gain and cost, ten seeds} Ten seeds are available for the three full-grid cities, New Delhi, Buenos Aires and London (Table~\ref{tab:seeds10b}). DIP reaches 1.23, 1.07 and 1.14 of the best grid point's gain in the three cities. It uses 5.7\%, 6.8\% and 8.3\% of a 63-point grid's calls.

\paragraph{Against the social optimum, ten seeds} The best grid point reaches 0.815, 0.938 and 0.905 of the social optimum's gain in the same three cities. DIP reaches 1.00, 1.00 and 1.02. Values above one occur where DIP's multi-start target is better than the benchmark. The grid's shortfall is the price of its two-parameter family. DIP's near-optimality is by construction the quality of its social target.

\paragraph{Gain and cost, seeds 0--2} In the same three full-grid cities, three seeds give 104--135\% of the grid's gain with 4.9--9.4\% of its calls (105--221 calls against 1\,921--2\,419). In Cairo no charge improves on the unpriced equilibrium.

\paragraph{Equilibrium check, seeds 0--2} In all 27 runs the cutting-plane loop ended with charges under which the target is an exact pure equilibrium (fresh-oracle regret at most $9.1\cdot10^{-13}$ USD). The tangent charge alone sufficed in 18. The uniform subsidy is 0.37--0.50 of the fee, and the zone charges follow the competitor-count rule of Proposition~\ref{prop:uniform}.

\paragraph{Qualifiers, cost} The per-run detail for seeds 0--2 is in Table~\ref{tab:dip} and Figure~\ref{fig:dip} (Appendix~F). Against the reduced exact grids, DIP uses more than 10\% of their calls in most runs (Cairo 8.5--12\%, Moscow 13\%, New York 23\%, Mumbai 24--31\%, Paris 21--23\%, Berlin 15--17\%).

\paragraph{Qualifiers, equilibrium selection} Wall-clock time is not always lower (median ratio 0.22; London seed 0: 247 s against 157 s). Selection is optimistic: under DIP's charges the grid's protocol reaches the target in 21 of 27 runs, and in 210 enumerated tiny games the equilibrium under DIP's charges was unique in only 35\% of the games. In the tiny games DIP reached the exact leader optimum over all zone charges in 93\% (100\% with the exact social optimum as target), so each miss was a miss of the social solver.

\paragraph{Qualifiers, $\Gamma$-maximin} With $\Gamma$-maximin carriers, the shifted charges of Theorem~\ref{thm:credal} kept the target an exact equilibrium in every run at $\delta=0.2$ and $0.8$ (largest regret $9.1\cdot10^{-13}$ USD), but at $\delta=0.8$ the required uniform subsidy is 0.98--3.6 times the fee, which is not a realistic policy.

\subsubsection{ARP: charges from observed aggregates}\label{sec:res-arp}

ARP was run with the exact oracle for ten seeds in New Delhi, Buenos Aires and London and five in Mumbai. The carriers best-respond day to day from their current plans after each posted charge, and all their oracle calls are counted; the status-quo equilibrium (40--60 calls) is common to all methods and not counted, as for DIP. Table~\ref{tab:arp} gives the seeds 0--2 runs in detail.

\paragraph{City runs, seeds 0--2} ARP reaches 90.8\% to 293\% of the grid's gain (mean 125\%) after 3--7 posted charges and 22--67 calls. DIP reaches 104--325\% (mean 134\%) with 105--221 calls. In the three full-grid cities the three-seed figures are 90.8--126\% for ARP and 104--135\% for DIP.

\paragraph{City runs, ten seeds} In the three full-grid cities over ten seeds, ARP's gain is 0.933, 0.995 and 0.926 of the social optimum's gain (Table~\ref{tab:seeds10b}). That is 0.93, 0.99 and 0.91 of DIP's gain. ARP reached an exact fixed point in 15 of 30 runs.

\paragraph{Where ARP falls short, seeds 0--2} ARP matches DIP within two percentage points in 5 of 12 runs. In the other 7 it is below DIP by up to 32 points of the grid's gain. The largest gaps occur where the iteration cycles or the grid is reduced. The clear miss is New Delhi seed 0 (90.8\% against DIP's 123\%), where the iteration cycles between two equilibria and $\varrho=0.40$, the largest of the sample.

\paragraph{Convergence, seeds 0--2} The flat rule reached an exact fixed point in 6 runs and cycled in 6. Successive averages do not remove the cycles. The relative change of the charge vector falls to 0.37--0.63 of its initial value after the first round and to 0.044--0.24 after the second.

\paragraph{Marginal tariff, seeds 0--2} The marginal tariff with the carriers' own best responses reaches 98.7--293\% (mean 125\%) with 8--14 calls and no city-side computation, and never raised the social cost along the way (12 of 12 runs), as Theorem~\ref{thm:mct} says; with the potential solver it reaches 92.5--263\% with 39--51 calls. Every returned profile is an exact pure equilibrium of the posted charges (regret at most $1.1\cdot10^{-13}$ USD for ARP and $1.4\cdot10^{-14}$ USD for the tariff). ARP's wall-clock time is 1.9--41 s, a median of 0.041 of DIP's.

\begin{figure}[tbp]\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.65\textwidth]{fig02_arp_calls_vs_gain.png}
\caption{Leader methods on four cities and three seeds (exact oracle): gain relative to the best exact grid point against routing-oracle calls (log scale).}\label{fig:arp}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{Tiny games and contraction constants} On 210 exhaustively enumerated games (136 with a positive leader gain; Table~\ref{tab:arp-tiny}, Appendix) the flat rule reached a fixed point in 166 games and cycled in 44, almost all at strong congestion; its mean share of the exact optimal gain is 0.72 / 0.64 / 0.23 at congestion multipliers 1, 20, 60 (tariff 0.93 / 0.79 / 0.58, DIP 1.00 / 0.96 / 0.93). Its failures are entry into zones that the optimum leaves unserved, attracted by the subsidy based on the city-mean lot, and two-charge cycles. Knowing the lots does not help (share 0.63): the limit is what the aggregate says about which profile is socially good (Theorem~\ref{thm:pure}(c)). Under the tariff the pure equilibria are exactly the unilateral social optima (the best tariff equilibrium is the social optimum in 210 of 210 games). On 48 convexified tiny games with $\varrho$ from 0.0062 to 0.81 the Lipschitz bound of Theorem~\ref{thm:resolvent}, the plain-step bound and the damped-step bound of Theorem~\ref{thm:rate} (tight, observed maximum 1.00 of the bound) held in all games. ARP is not better than DIP in solution quality; it is better in the information it needs and in cost.

\subsection{Robustness to the carriers' criterion}\label{sec:res-crit}

All game results above describe carriers who plan against the p-box worst-case route cost. To test how much this matters we recomputed the unpriced equilibrium, the price of anarchy, DIP and a coarse 16-point grid for carriers who plan with the sample-average cost of the same 30 days, with a Wasserstein ball with cross-validated radius, or with the theory radius, for all nine cities and 3 seeds. The SAA and Wasserstein carriers use a heuristic oracle without an optimality proof (equilibria verified against an independent fresh pool, regret at most $5\cdot10^{-13}$); the p-box reference uses price-and-branch; the coarse grid is easier to match than the 63-point grid; the cross-validated Wasserstein criterion was run for one seed only in Paris and Berlin. Table~\ref{tab:criterion} gives the three low-service cities.

Weighted DIP charge: $u_z/$fee weighted by deliveries.}\label{tab:criterion}

\paragraph{What is robust} Under all four criteria: the delivery charge alone achieves 0.88--1.0 of the best grid gain (0.74--1.00 for the theory radius); the congestion charge alone is small, though marginal for SAA in Buenos Aires (3.3\% against 18\% for the delivery charge); the price of anarchy is at least 1.10 only in London, Buenos Aires and New Delhi (at most 1.06 elsewhere with SAA carriers); DIP's per-delivery charge follows $-0.375\,$fee$/n_z$ (slopes 0.361--0.379, correlation at least 0.984 in absolute value); DIP matches the coarse-grid gain (ratio at least 0.99) with 5.0--10\% of the calls of a 63-point grid (21--43\% of the coarse grid's); the optimal charge is a subsidy in every city; and cities with complete service are unchanged.

\paragraph{What is not robust} The levels. With SAA carriers the unpriced served share roughly doubles (London 0.18 to 0.38, Buenos Aires 0.37 to 0.67, New Delhi 0.26 to 0.48) and the price of anarchy measured with the true cost falls (London 2.06 to 1.52, Buenos Aires 1.49 to 1.16, New Delhi 1.35 to 1.19); the model-cost price of anarchy moves little. The ordering of the price of anarchy is not preserved: with SAA carriers it is 1.16 and 1.19 in Buenos Aires and New Delhi. The leader's gain under the true cost falls by half or more (London 108\% to 51.1\%, Buenos Aires 51.2\% to 16.0\%, New Delhi 34.0\% to 18.4\%), and the best uniform charge for the true-cost objective shrinks by 58--69\% (p-box 0.80, 0.77, 0.53 of the fee, beyond the $-0.6$ end of the grid axis, against 0.33, 0.30, 0.17 for SAA), while DIP's zone charges themselves are almost unchanged. \emph{All levels of the price of anarchy, served share and gain in this paper describe carriers planning against a guaranteed upper bound; the orderings and the policy directions are robust, the levels are not.}

\subsection{Theory against data}\label{sec:res-theory}

The city games are of type D1, so the engine's profile is an exact pure equilibrium with E-threshold zero and Theorem~\ref{thm:main} holds trivially for it. To test the theorems non-trivially we computed the exact thresholds of Theorem~\ref{thm:cert} on mixed equilibria with belief weights equal to the realised aggregate (price-and-branch oracle; each threshold is relaxed by the largest improvement observed at a verified equilibrium). Table~\ref{tab:theory} and Figure~\ref{fig:theory} are in Appendix~F.

\paragraph{Credal absorption} Non-trivial mixed equilibria were constructed from pairs of distinct pure equilibria by solving the two-plan indifference conditions and checking that every support plan is a best response over all plans. This succeeded in 14 of the 27 city-seed games; Cairo, Moscow have none, because almost all demand is served. On all 274 tested profiles no statement of Theorem~\ref{thm:main} was violated (0 violations). The bound is loose: the largest empirical threshold is 0.28--0.60 times the anchored bound (median 0.45). As Remark~\ref{rem:regime} states, on these games $N\le6<d$, so the $(N-1)\bar\rho$ term is the smallest of the three in every case; the $N$-free terms are not tested by the city data. Without the best-response polish, the rounded profile of the first engine phase is E-admissible at a finite width in only 10 of 27 games. Limits: mixed equilibria were found only with two-plan supports, and thresholds are exact only up to the oracle gap.

\paragraph{$\Gamma$-maximin} In all 108 (city, seed, $\delta$) cases the surcharge equilibrium differs from the precise equilibrium, and in 106 of them the precise equilibrium is not $\Gamma$-admissible. The share of demand served drops by 0--0.082 at $\delta=0.1$ and by 0.17--0.77 at $\delta=1$.

\paragraph{Vehicle-count congestion} The gadgets behave as proved: Proposition~\ref{prop:tight} has no pure equilibrium and threshold exactly $1/w$ (3 of 3 weights), and the kernel construction reproduces the kernel sets for all four criteria on 223 of 223 digraphs. Among random bilinear D2 games with three players, 6 of 400 have no pure equilibrium, against 0 of 150 D1 controls. Small routing-integrated D2 games always had a pure equilibrium (0 of 540), and on the city games of Cairo, Mumbai and Paris with a vehicle-count externality scaled by 1, 10, 100, 1\,000, 10\,000, all 180 best-response runs converged. Non-existence required adversarial plan menus; the D2 calibration is an assumption introduced for this test.

\subsection{Verification}\label{sec:res-verif}

On 40 tiny games solved by exhaustive enumeration, the engine's profile is a pure equilibrium in every case and reaches the global potential minimum in 40 of 40 (best response 35, Frank--Wolfe with rounding 38, column generation 39; Table~\ref{tab:tiny}). At city scale every base equilibrium, every verified grid best point (27 city-seeds) and every equilibrium of the price-of-anarchy experiment has regret at most $9.1\cdot10^{-13}$ USD against an independent fresh exact oracle. Because some oracle calls were not proven optimal, the guaranteed regret bound is nonzero for 10 profiles, at most 4.84 USD. These checks rely on the same branch-and-price method as the oracle; the pricer was checked against enumeration (Section~\ref{sec:res-brl}).

\section{Managerial and smart-city implications}\label{sec:implications}

The model is stylised and several parameters are weak (Section~\ref{sec:data}), so the effects below are qualitative: they indicate what to test with local data, not what to do in any particular city.

\subsection{Managerial implications}

\paragraph{Where pricing matters} In the model, the efficiency loss from competition is concentrated where service is incomplete. Where carriers already serve almost all demand (Cairo, Moscow, New York, Paris, Berlin), the price of anarchy is close to one and charges change little; kerb space and background traffic are the more relevant levers there. Where service is partial, the instrument with the largest effect is a per-delivery subsidy. In every material case the best flat charge was a subsidy, and it achieves 0.82--0.98 of the gain of a two-instrument charge in the three cities with a full grid. A congestion charge on vehicle-hours alone gains much less (mean 0.32--2.4\% against 12.1--39.8\%) but not nothing: it reached at least 1.7\% in 7 of 30 city-seeds. The gains depend strongly on the sampled slice and the ordering of the cities by price of anarchy holds in 7 of 10 seeds only, so the direction is better supported than the size. A subsidy has a fiscal cost that the welfare criterion with transfers does not count, access stays unequal in the low-service cities, and an objective that counts delay to other road users can reverse the recommendation.

\paragraph{How a city could compute charges} A city does not need to search a grid. With the carriers' routing oracle, DIP gives zone charges at 5.7--8.3\% of a 63-point grid's calls, with a readable structure: a subsidy of roughly 37.5\% of the fee divided by the number of carriers in the zone, corrected for congestion. Without the oracle, ARP needs only the zone totals after each posted charge, and the marginal tariff needs billing on own deliveries and the zone total. Observation-only pricing carries selection risk: the flat rule can cycle or settle in a worse local optimum, and the tariff stops at a unilateral social optimum. A pilot with a few posted charges is a cheap check, and zone-specific charges are harder to administer than a flat subsidy.

\paragraph{Levels depend on how carriers plan} All levels in this paper describe carriers who plan against a guaranteed upper bound on route cost. With sample-average carriers the sign of the subsidy, the dominance of the delivery charge and the structure of DIP's charges persist, but service at the unpriced equilibrium roughly doubles, the leader's gain falls by half or more and the best subsidy for the true-cost objective shrinks by 58--69\%. A city should therefore calibrate the level of any charge on observed carrier behaviour and treat our magnitudes as indicative.

\paragraph{For carriers and for modelling} Wider travel-time boxes and wider beliefs about rivals reduce the volume a carrier finds worth serving. A carrier with enough data to estimate the law should weigh that the p-box criterion costs expected cost (1.6--8.9\% at up to 60 training days) in exchange for tail protection and a bound. For a modeller, exposure-based congestion gives a potential game with guaranteed pure equilibria, whereas vehicle-count congestion can make existence NP-complete; on our instances that worst case did not occur.

\subsection{Relevance for data-driven smart-city logistics}

The study is an offline simulation on open data. It contains no machine learning, digital twin, real-time control or experiment with decision makers. Within that limit it speaks to the following themes.

\paragraph{Data-driven optimisation under data uncertainty} The instances are built from open data (OpenStreetMap, gridded population) and public parameters, and each carrier's uncertainty set is built from 30 observed days of travel times, so the models run on data of the kind cities and carriers collect. The probability-box criterion trades expected cost for a guaranteed upper bound and tail protection (Section~\ref{sec:res-pbox}); it suits settings in which a guarantee matters more than the average.

\paragraph{Urban logistics and public--private coordination} The public leader sets charges and the private carriers respond. DIP and the marginal tariff let the city compute zone charges that make a chosen social outcome stable, and ARP needs only zone totals, the information that a reporting or billing arrangement between city and carriers could supply. Because equilibrium selection is optimistic, a pilot should check that carriers reach the intended outcome.

\paragraph{Implementable, human-centred decisions} The charges are simple to state: a per-delivery subsidy of about 37.5\% of the fee divided by the number of competitors in a zone, plus non-negative congestion charges. The model does not represent fairness across carriers or neighbourhoods, and we did not involve carriers or officials. Whether the methods work with a human in the loop, on live data or in a simulation environment of the city is not tested here; ARP's loop of posting a charge, observing the totals and updating is the form such a pilot would take.

\section{Limitations}\label{sec:limits}

\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*,itemsep=2pt]
\item \emph{Model.} Lots are all-or-nothing (S1), so part of the measured inefficiency may be an artifact of indivisibility. Congestion is linear and, within a zone, proportional to deliveries (S3, S4). Demand is sampled, market shares are equal in seven cities, and the congestion coupling is scaled by a slice multiplicity. Most operating parameters are country-level or rough; delivery fee, drops per vehicle and the number of carriers move welfare by tens of per cent over their plausible ranges (Table~\ref{tab:sens}), and the sign of the best per-delivery instrument depends on the fee-to-cost ratio.
\item \emph{Exactness and coverage.} 344 of 41{,}469 oracle calls were not proven optimal; for 10 profiles the guaranteed regret bound is positive (at most 4.84 USD). The grid is exact in full only for New Delhi, Buenos Aires, London, so the grid's gains in the other six cities are lower bounds; imprecision sweeps in Cairo, Moscow, New York, Paris, Berlin use three exact widths per sweep; the engine comparison, ZMC checks, theory tests and tiny-game verification use price-and-branch, whose equilibria were not exact in 10 of 27 city-seeds. Branch-and-price was tested only on instances with 22 customer zones.
\item \emph{Carriers' criterion and BRL.} All levels describe carriers who plan against the p-box upper bound. The robustness study used a heuristic oracle for sample-average and Wasserstein carriers, a coarse 16-point grid, three seeds, and price-and-branch for the p-box reference (Section~\ref{sec:res-crit}). The out-of-sample comparison of criteria uses one slice per city and a heuristic planner validated against branch-and-price in nine instances. BRL does not beat sample-average or Wasserstein plans on held-out cost, was not compared with resource-extension-function dominance, and was benchmarked on public instances only by an emulation (soft duration limits, four sectors), not by reproducing published results.
\item \emph{Leader methods.} DIP is no better than its social target, its selection is optimistic, its closed forms need D1 and one hours-per-delivery ratio, and the subsidies it requires under strong ambiguity aversion are unrealistic. ARP was run on four cities, can cycle, can settle in a worse local optimum, and has no guarantee for the pure game unless its fixed point is reached; Theorem~\ref{thm:pure}(b) is conditional on potential minimisation. Neither method has a worst-equilibrium guarantee. The comparison with the grid is between a two-parameter family and zone-specific charges.
\item \emph{Theory.} The credal results assume affine coupling. The absorption tests rely on two-plan mixed equilibria and thresholds exact up to the oracle gap; the full rounding algorithm was not run on real data; D2 was tested only with a calibration introduced for the test. The Lean formalisation covers only the finite and algebraic cores listed in Appendix~\ref{app:lean}.
\item \emph{Evidence and novelty checks.} Seeds vary the sampled slice, not the calibration inputs. Ten seeds exist only for New Delhi, Buenos Aires, London; the other cities have 9 seeds for equilibria and three seeds with a reduced grid for grid-derived quantities (lower bounds), the imprecision sweeps and the criterion study use three seeds, and ARP was run in four cities. Price-of-anarchy values come from at most six equilibria per city and seed. The broad social cost is driven by a proxy and supports no conclusion. Novelty searches were web and index searches, partly at the level of abstracts, and a Scopus and Web of Science search has not been done. The closest neighbours \citep{GhosalHoWiesemann2024,Masiha2026,Feik2026,Beauchene2016,LiuMaMa2026,Irnich2008} were read in full text (the first in its October 2021 preprint version, Beauch\^ene's paper as main text and Liu--Ma--Ma as pp.\ 1--13). \citet{Lanzetti2025}, the trial-and-error toll papers, the budgeted-uncertainty pricing papers, the function-label papers \citep{Dabia2013,Ioachim1998} and the other neighbours were read in part or at abstract level.
\end{itemize}
Open questions include the complexity of finding a pure E-admissible equilibrium above the guaranteed width (we conjecture PPAD-hardness), smoothness constants for the Cournot--congestion game, an absorption result for nonaffine congestion, worst-equilibrium guarantees for the leader, and carrier-level data that would replace our proxies.

\section{Conclusion}\label{sec:conclusion}

A city that prices competing delivery carriers faces NP-hard best responses under probability-box travel times, a congestion game among the carriers and a bilevel search over charges. We gave an algorithm for each layer and tested them on nine metropolitan areas.

\emph{Carriers' routing.} BRL prices routes exactly under the dependence-robust criterion and, with branch-and-price, proved optimal robust plans on all nine metro instances with 22 customer zones. Its window rule keeps fewer labels than weaker rules, but the gain shrinks on real data, the plans do not beat sample-average plans out of sample, and exact solution is shown only up to 22 customer zones in all nine cities and 30 to 40 customers in London and New York.

\emph{City charges.} DIP made its target an exact equilibrium in every run and reached 1.07--1.23 times the best grid point's gain with 5.7--8.3\% of its calls in the three cities with ten seeds, partly because its charges are zone-specific; against the social optimum the grid reaches 0.815--0.938 and DIP 1.00--1.02. ARP, which sees only zone totals, reached 0.933--0.995 of the social optimum's gain and contracts at a rate independent of instance size in the convexified game; in the pure game it can cycle or settle in a worse local optimum.

\emph{Theory.} Pure equilibria exist under exposure-based congestion and deciding existence is strongly NP-complete under vehicle-count congestion. The credal absorption width has an $(N-1)$ term that is attained at $N=2$ and binds on the city instances, and the theorem is close to earlier work on approximate equilibria.

\emph{Cities.} The price of anarchy is near one where service is complete and larger where it is partial, with a wide spread over seeds and an ordering that holds in 7 of 10 seeds. A per-delivery subsidy captures most of the grid leader's gain; a congestion charge alone captures much less but sometimes more than 1.7\%. These levels describe carriers who plan against a guaranteed upper bound: with sample-average carriers service roughly doubles and the leader's gain falls by half or more, while orderings and policy directions persist. The findings come from stylised slices with weak parameters, and the novelty of each component is limited as stated in Section~\ref{sec:intro}. The observation-only methods, ARP and the marginal tariff, are the natural candidates for a pilot with real data.
